203:          # Exit to privilege exec mode then save
204:          con.acquire_priv("privilege_exec")
205:          con.send_command("write memory")
206: +
207: +        # After the save: every 'username' line flags its user again, including
208: +        # the ones a startup configuration brings, and the console authentication
209: +        # this needs is only ever meant to live in the running configuration.
210: +        self.complete_first_login(con)
211: +
212:          self.logger.debug("Closing connection")
213:          con.close()
214:  
215: +    def complete_first_login(self, con):
216: +        """Answer the password change ASA 9.18+ forces at the first login.
217: +
218: +        Those releases mark a local user whose password was set by an administrator
219: +        as "New-User" ('show aaa local user') and make the first authenticated login
220: +        change it. Nothing clears that flag from the configuration, and the console
221: +        is not authenticated, so the dialog normally waits for the first SSH login --
222: +        which this container cannot make itself: with management passthrough the
223: +        management address sits on its own eth0 and the VM only ever sees what tc
224: +        redirects to tap0. The console is put behind local authentication for the
225: +        length of one login instead, and the dialog answered there.
226: +
227: +        The password is set to the one the user already has: the point is only to
228: +        clear the flag so that the first real login lands on a prompt instead of the
229: +        dialog. Releases that do not force the change simply log in.
230: +
231: +        A failure here only leaves the dialog to whoever logs in first, so it is
232: +        logged and the bootstrap carries on.
233: +        """
234: +        exec_prompt = re.compile(rf"{re.escape(self.hostname)}>")
235: +        priv_prompt = re.compile(rf"{re.escape(self.hostname)}#")
236: +        answers = [
237: +            (re.compile(r"[Uu]sername:"), self.username),
238: +            (re.compile(r"[Ee]nter old password"), self.password),
239: +            (re.compile(r"[Ee]nter new password"), self.password),
240: +            (re.compile(r"[Cc]onfirm new password"), self.password),
241: +            (re.compile(r"[Pp]assword:"), self.password),
242: +        ]
243: +
244: +        self.logger.debug("Handling the forced password change at first login")
245: +        try:
246: +            con.send_configs(["aaa authentication serial console LOCAL"])
247: +            # send_configs leaves the session in configuration mode, where 'exit'
248: +            # only drops back to the enable prompt instead of logging out
249: +            con.acquire_priv("privilege_exec")
250: +            self._console_dialog(con, "exit", exec_prompt, answers)
251: +            self._console_dialog(con, "enable", priv_prompt, answers)
252: +            con._current_priv_level = con.privilege_levels["privilege_exec"]
253: +            con.send_configs(["no aaa authentication serial console LOCAL"])
254: +        except (ScrapliException, OSError) as exc:
255: +            self.logger.warning(
256: +                f"Could not complete the first login ({exc}), the password change "
257: +                "is left to whoever logs in to this device first"
258: +            )
259: +            # best effort, so that a console is not left behind a login prompt
260: +            try:
261: +                con._current_priv_level = con.privilege_levels["privilege_exec"]
262: +                con.send_configs(["no aaa authentication serial console LOCAL"])
263: +            except (ScrapliException, OSError):
264: +                self.logger.warning(
265: +                    f"The console of this device is left behind a login prompt, "
266: +                    f"use {self.username} to get past it"
267: +                )
268: +
269: +    def _console_dialog(self, con, command, prompt, answers):
270: +        """Send a command on the console and answer prompts until 'prompt' shows up
271: +
272: +        The prompts an ASA offers vary from release to release, so they are
273: +        answered as they come rather than in a fixed order, and a release that
274: +        skips one of them simply lands on 'prompt' without ever being asked.
275: +        """
276: +        timeout = vrnetlab.getenv_uint("CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT", CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT)
277: +        # A read that times out takes the transport down with it, so it has to be
278: +        # kept short enough to not stall the boot for the whole transport timeout.
279: +        # 'con' commandeered the transport, which kept the timeouts of the
280: +        # connection it came from: setting them on 'con' would have no effect.
281: +        transport_args = con.transport._base_transport_args
282: +        original_timeout = transport_args.timeout_transport
283: +        transport_args.timeout_transport = timeout
284: +
285: +        con.channel.write(command)
286: +        con.channel.send_return()
287: +
288: +        buf = ""
289: +        deadline = time.time() + timeout
290: +        try:
291: +            while time.time() < deadline:
292: +                buf += con.channel.read().decode(errors="replace")
293: +                if prompt.search(buf):
294: +                    return
295: +                # the earliest match wins: 'Enter Password:' ends in 'Password:'
296: +                # too, and the generic prompt would otherwise swallow it
297: +                matched = [
298: +                    (match.start(), match.end(), reply)
299: +                    for pattern, reply in answers
300: +                    for match in [pattern.search(buf)]
301: +                    if match
302: +                ]
303: +                if not matched:
304: +                    continue
305: +                _, end, reply = min(matched)
306: +                buf = buf[end:]
307: +                con.channel.write(reply, redacted=True)
308: +                con.channel.send_return()
309: +
310: +            raise ScrapliTimeout(f"no '{prompt.pattern}' prompt after '{command}'")
311: +        finally:
312: +            transport_args.timeout_transport = original_timeout
313: +
314:  
315:  class ASAv(vrnetlab.VR):
316:      def __init__(self, username, password, conn_mode, hostname):
317:          super(ASAv, self).__init__(username, password)
318:          self.vms = [ASAv_vm(username, password, conn_mode, hostname)]

51: class ASAv_vm(vrnetlab.VM):
52:     def __init__(self, username, password, conn_mode, hostname, install_mode=False):
53:         for e in os.listdir("/"):
54:             if re.search(".qcow2$", e):
55:                 disk_image = "/" + e
56: 
57:         super(ASAv_vm, self).__init__(
58:             username,
59:             password,
60:             disk_image=disk_image,
61:             ram=2048,
62:             cpu="Nehalem",
63:         )
64:         self.hostname = hostname
65:         self.nic_type = "e1000"
66:         self.conn_mode = conn_mode
67:         self.install_mode = install_mode
68:         self.num_nics = 8
69: 
70:     def bootstrap_spin(self):
71:         """This function should be called periodically to do work."""
72: 
73:         if self.spins > 300:
74:             # too many spins with no result ->  give up
75:             self.stop()
76:             self.start()
77:             return
78: 
79:         (ridx, match, res) = self.con_expect([b"ciscoasa>"], 1)
80:         if match:  # got a match!
81:             if ridx == 0:  # login
82:                 if self.install_mode:
83:                     self.logger.debug("matched, ciscoasa>")
84:                     self.wait_write("", wait=None)
85:                     self.wait_write("", None)
86:                     self.wait_write("", wait="ciscoasa>")
87:                     self.running = True
88:                     return
89: 
90:                 self.logger.debug("matched, ciscoasa>")
91:                 self.wait_write("", wait=None)
92: 
93:                 # run main config!
94:                 self.apply_config()
95: 
96:                 # startup time?
97:                 startup_time = datetime.datetime.now() - self.start_time
98:                 self.logger.debug("Startup complete in: %s" % startup_time)
99:                 # mark as running
100:                 self.running = True
101:                 return
102: 
103:         # no match, if we saw some output from the router it's probably
104:         # booting, so let's give it some more time
105:         if res != b"":
106:             self.write_to_stdout(res)
107:             # reset spins if we saw some output
108:             self.spins = 0
109: 
110:         self.spins += 1
111: 
112:         return
113: 
114:     def apply_config(self):
115:         """Apply the full configuration"""
116:         self.logger.debug("Applying bootstrap configuration")
117: 
118:         scrapli_timeout = vrnetlab.getenv_uint(
119:             "SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT", vrnetlab.DEFAULT_SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT
120:         )
121: 
122:         def _open(conn):
123:             """Set the internal privilege level to 'exec' so scrapli knows what to do"""
124:             conn._current_priv_level = conn.privilege_levels["exec"]
125:             self.logger.debug(
126:                 "Set initial privilege level to 'exec' to boostrap configuration"
127:             )
128: 
129:         asa_scrapli_dev = {
130:             "platform": "cisco_asa",
131:             "host": "127.0.0.1",
132:             "auth_bypass": True,
133:             "auth_strict_key": False,
134:             "auth_secondary": self.password,
135:             "timeout_socket": scrapli_timeout,
136:             "timeout_transport": scrapli_timeout,
137:             "timeout_ops": scrapli_timeout,
138:             "on_open": _open,
139:         }
140: 
141:         con = Scrapli(**asa_scrapli_dev)
142:         con.commandeer(conn=self.scrapli_tn)
143: 
144:         # On a fresh ASA, typing 'enable' prompts to set the password up -- but
145:         # only since 9.12 or so. Older releases just authenticate against the
146:         # empty password they ship with and go straight to the enable prompt,
147:         # where 'aaa authentication enable console LOCAL' takes over below.
148:         self.logger.debug("Setting up initial enable password")
149:         self._console_dialog(
150:             con,
151:             "enable",
152:             re.compile(r"ciscoasa#"),
153:             [
154:                 (re.compile(r"Enter\s+Password:"), self.password),
155:                 (re.compile(r"Repeat\s+Password:"), self.password),
156:                 (re.compile(r"Password:"), ""),
157:             ],
158:         )
159: 
160:         # Releases that do not report anonymously never ask the question
161:         self.logger.debug("Entering configuration mode to handle reporting prompt")
162:         self._console_dialog(
163:             con,
164:             "configure terminal",
165:             re.compile(r"\(config\)#"),
166:             [(re.compile(r"Would you like to enable anonymous error reporting"), "N")],
167:         )
168: 
169:         v4_mgmt_address = vrnetlab.cidr_to_ddn(self.mgmt_address_ipv4)
170:         ipv6_address = self.render_optional_mgmt_config(
171:             "ipv6 address {address}", address=self.mgmt_address_ipv6
172:         )
173:         ipv6_route = self.render_optional_mgmt_config(
174:             "route management ::/0 {gateway} 1", gateway=self.mgmt_gw_ipv6
175:         )
176: 
177:         config_commands = f"""hostname {self.hostname}
178: aaa authentication ssh console LOCAL
179: aaa authentication enable console LOCAL
180: username {self.username} password {self.password} privilege 15
181: interface Management0/0
182: nameif management
183: security-level 100
184: ip address {v4_mgmt_address[0]} {v4_mgmt_address[1]}
185: {ipv6_address}
186: no shutdown
187: exit
188: route management 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 {self.mgmt_gw_ipv4} 1
189: {ipv6_route}
190: access-list MGMT_IN extended permit tcp any any eq ssh
191: access-group MGMT_IN in interface management
192: crypto key generate ecdsa elliptic-curve 256
193: ssh key-exchange group dh-group14-sha256
194: ssh 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 management
195: ssh ::/0 management
196: no ssh stricthostkeycheck
197: ssh timeout 60"""
198: 
199:         self.logger.debug("Sending configuration commands")
200:         con.send_configs(config_commands.splitlines())
201: 
202:         # Apply user-provided startup configuration if present
203:         if os.path.exists(STARTUP_CONFIG_FILE):
204:             self.logger.info("Startup configuration file found")
205:             with open(STARTUP_CONFIG_FILE, "r") as config:
206:                 startup_config = config.read()
207:                 self.logger.debug("Applying startup configuration")
208:                 con.send_configs(startup_config.splitlines())
209:         else:
210:             self.logger.info("User provided startup configuration is not found.")
211: 
212:         self.logger.debug("Saving configuration")
213:         # Exit to privilege exec mode then save
214:         con.acquire_priv("privilege_exec")
215:         con.send_command("write memory")
216: 
217:         # After the save: every 'username' line flags its user again, including
218:         # the ones a startup configuration brings, and the console authentication
219:         # this needs is only ever meant to live in the running configuration.
220:         self.complete_first_login(con)
221: 
222:         self.logger.debug("Closing connection")
223:         con.close()
224: 
225:     def complete_first_login(self, con):
226:         """Answer the password change ASA 9.18+ forces at the first login.
227: 
228:         Those releases mark a local user whose password was set by an administrator
229:         as "New-User" ('show aaa local user') and make the first authenticated login
230:         change it. Nothing clears that flag from the configuration, and the console
231:         is not authenticated, so the dialog normally waits for the first SSH login --
232:         which this container cannot make itself: with management passthrough the
233:         management address sits on its own eth0 and the VM only ever sees what tc
234:         redirects to tap0. The console is put behind local authentication for the
235:         length of one login instead, and the dialog answered there.
236: 
237:         The password is set to the one the user already has: the point is only to
238:         clear the flag so that the first real login lands on a prompt instead of the
239:         dialog. Releases that do not force the change simply log in.
240: 
241:         A failure here only leaves the dialog to whoever logs in first, so it is
242:         logged and the bootstrap carries on.
243:         """
244:         exec_prompt = re.compile(rf"{re.escape(self.hostname)}>")
245:         priv_prompt = re.compile(rf"{re.escape(self.hostname)}#")
246:         answers = [
247:             (re.compile(r"[Uu]sername:"), self.username),
248:             (re.compile(r"[Ee]nter old password"), self.password),
249:             (re.compile(r"[Ee]nter new password"), self.password),
250:             (re.compile(r"[Cc]onfirm new password"), self.password),
251:             (re.compile(r"[Pp]assword:"), self.password),
252:         ]
253: 
254:         self.logger.debug("Handling the forced password change at first login")
255:         try:
256:             con.send_configs(["aaa authentication serial console LOCAL"])
257:             # send_configs leaves the session in configuration mode, where 'exit'
258:             # only drops back to the enable prompt instead of logging out
259:             con.acquire_priv("privilege_exec")
260:             self._console_dialog(con, "exit", exec_prompt, answers)
261:             self._console_dialog(con, "enable", priv_prompt, answers)
262:             con._current_priv_level = con.privilege_levels["privilege_exec"]
263:             con.send_configs(["no aaa authentication serial console LOCAL"])
264:         except (ScrapliException, OSError) as exc:
265:             self.logger.warning(
266:                 f"Could not complete the first login ({exc}), the password change "
267:                 "is left to whoever logs in to this device first"
268:             )
269:             # best effort, so that a console is not left behind a login prompt
270:             try:
271:                 con._current_priv_level = con.privilege_levels["privilege_exec"]
272:                 con.send_configs(["no aaa authentication serial console LOCAL"])
273:             except (ScrapliException, OSError):
274:                 self.logger.warning(
275:                     f"The console of this device is left behind a login prompt, "
276:                     f"use {self.username} to get past it"
277:                 )
278: 
279:     def _console_dialog(self, con, command, prompt, answers):
280:         """Send a command on the console and answer prompts until 'prompt' shows up
281: 
282:         The prompts an ASA offers vary from release to release, so they are
283:         answered as they come rather than in a fixed order, and a release that
284:         skips one of them simply lands on 'prompt' without ever being asked.
285:         """
286:         timeout = vrnetlab.getenv_uint("CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT", CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT)
287:         # A read that times out takes the transport down with it, so it has to be
288:         # kept short enough to not stall the boot for the whole transport timeout.
289:         # 'con' commandeered the transport, which kept the timeouts of the
290:         # connection it came from: setting them on 'con' would have no effect.
291:         transport_args = con.transport._base_transport_args
292:         original_timeout = transport_args.timeout_transport
293:         transport_args.timeout_transport = timeout
294: 
295:         con.channel.write(command)
296:         con.channel.send_return()
297: 
298:         buf = ""
299:         deadline = time.time() + timeout
300:         try:
301:             while time.time() < deadline:
302:                 buf += con.channel.read().decode(errors="replace")
303:                 if prompt.search(buf):
304:                     return
305:                 # the earliest match wins: 'Enter Password:' ends in 'Password:'
306:                 # too, and the generic prompt would otherwise swallow it
307:                 matched = [
308:                     (match.start(), match.end(), reply)
309:                     for pattern, reply in answers
310:                     for match in [pattern.search(buf)]
311:                     if match
312:                 ]
313:                 if not matched:
314:                     continue
315:                 _, end, reply = min(matched)
316:                 buf = buf[end:]
317:                 con.channel.write(reply, redacted=True)
318:                 con.channel.send_return()
319: 
320:             raise ScrapliTimeout(f"no '{prompt.pattern}' prompt after '{command}'")
321:         finally:
322:             transport_args.timeout_transport = original_timeout
323: 
324: 
325: class ASAv(vrnetlab.VR):
326:     def __init__(self, username, password, conn_mode, hostname):
327:         super(ASAv, self).__init__(username, password)
328:         self.vms = [ASAv_vm(username, password, conn_mode, hostname)]
329:

331: class ASAv_installer(ASAv):
332:     """ASAv installer"""
333: 
334:     def __init__(self, username, password, conn_mode, hostname):
335:         super(ASAv_installer, self).__init__(username, password, conn_mode, hostname)
336:         self.vms = [ASAv_vm(username, password, conn_mode, hostname, install_mode=True)]
337: 
338:     def install(self):
339:         self.logger.info("Installing ASAv")
340:         asav = self.vms[0]
341:         while not asav.running:
342:             asav.work()
343:         asav.stop()
344:         self.logger.info("Installation complete")
345:

Older releases just authenticate against the\n        # empty password they ship with and go straight to the enable prompt,\n        # where 'aaa authentication enable console LOCAL' takes over below.\n        self.logger.debug(\"Setting up initial enable password\")\n        self._console_dialog(\n            con,\n            \"enable\",\n            re.compile(r\"ciscoasa#\"),\n            [\n                (re.compile(r\"Enter\\s+Password:\"), self.password),\n                (re.compile(r\"Repeat\\s+Password:\"), self.password),\n                (re.compile(r\"Password:\"), \"\"),\n            ],\n        )\n\n        # Exit to privilege exec mode then save\n        con.acquire_priv(\"privilege_exec\")\n        con.send_command(\"write memory\")\n\n        # After the save: every 'username' line flags its user again, including\n        # Nothing clears that flag from the configuration, and the console\n        is not authenticated, so the dialog normally waits for the first SSH login --\n        which this container cannot make itself: with management passthrough the\n        management address sits on its own eth0 and the VM only ever sees what tc\n        redirects to tap0. The console is put behind local authentication for the\n        length of one login instead, and the dialog answered there.\n\n        The password is set to the one the user already has: the point is only to\n        clear the flag so that the first real login lands on a prompt instead of the\n        dialog. Releases that do not force the change simply log in.\n\n        A failure here only leaves the dialog to whoever logs in first, so it is\n        logged and the bootstrap carries on.\n        \"\"\"\n serial console LOCAL\"])\n        except (ScrapliException, OSError) as exc:\n            self.logger.warning(\n                f\"Could not complete the first login ({exc}), the password change \"\n                \"is left to whoever logs in to this device first\"\n            )\n            # best effort, so that a console is not left behind a login prompt\n            try:\n                con._current_priv_level = con.privilege_levels[\"privilege_exec\"]\n                con.send_configs([\"no aaa authentication serial console LOCAL\"])\n            except (ScrapliException, OSError):\n                self.logger.warning(\n                    f\"The console of this device is left behind a login prompt, \"\n                    f\"use {self.username} to get past it\"\n                )\n\n    def _console_dialog(self, con, command, prompt, answers):\n        \"\"\"Send a command on the console and answer prompts until 'prompt' shows up\n\n        The prompts an ASA offers vary from release to release, so they are\n        answered as they come rather than in a fixed order, and a release that\n        skips one of them simply lands on 'prompt' without ever being asked.\n        \"\"\"\n        timeout = vrnetlab.getenv_uint(\"CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT\", CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT)\n        # A read that times out takes the transport down with it, so it has to be\n        # kept short enough to not stall the boot for the whole transport timeout.\n        # 'con' commandeered the transport, which kept the timeouts of the\n        # connection it came from: setting them on 'con' would have no effect.\n        transport_args = con.transport._base_transport_args\n        original_timeout = transport_args.timeout_transport\n        transport_args.timeout_transport = timeout\n\n        con.channel.write(command)\n        con.channel.send_return()\n\n        buf = \"\"\n        deadline = time.time() + timeout\n        try:\n            while time.time() < deadline:\n                buf += con.channel.read().decode(errors=\"replace\")\n                if prompt.search(buf):\n                    return\n                # the earliest match wins: 'Enter Password:' ends in 'Password:'\n                # too, and the generic prompt would otherwise swallow it\n                matched = [\n                    (match.start(), match.end(), reply)\n                    for pattern, reply in answers\n                    for match in [pattern.search(buf)]\n                    if match\n                ]\n                if not matched:\n                    continue\n                _, end, reply = min(matched)\n                buf = buf[end:]\n                con.channel.write(reply, redacted=True)\n                con.channel.send_return()\n\n            raise ScrapliTimeout(f\"no '{prompt.pattern}' prompt after '{command}'\")\n        finally:\n            transport_args.timeout_transport = original_timeout\n\n\nclass ASAv(vrnetlab.VR):\n    def 97:         # booting, so let's give it some more time
98:         if res != b"":
99:             self.write_to_stdout(res)
100:             # reset spins if we saw some output
101:             self.spins = 0
102: 
103:         self.spins += 1
104: 
105:         return
106: 
107:     def apply_config(self):
108:         """Apply the full configuration"""
109:         self.logger.debug("Applying bootstrap configuration")
110: 
111:         scrapli_timeout = vrnetlab.getenv_uint(
112:             "SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT", vrnetlab.DEFAULT_SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT
113:         )
114: 
115:         def _open(conn):
116:             """Set the internal privilege level to 'exec' so scrapli knows what to do"""
117:             conn._current_priv_level = conn.privilege_levels["exec"]
118:             self.logger.debug(
119:                 "Set initial privilege level to 'exec' to boostrap configuration"
120:             )
121: 
122:         asa_scrapli_dev = {
123:             "platform": "cisco_asa",
124:             "host": "127.0.0.1",
125:             "auth_bypass": True,
126:             "auth_strict_key": False,
127:             "auth_secondary": self.password,
128:             "timeout_socket": scrapli_timeout,
129:             "timeout_transport": scrapli_timeout,
130:             "timeout_ops": scrapli_timeout,
131:             "on_open": _open,
132:         }
133: 
134:         con = Scrapli(**asa_scrapli_dev)
135:         con.commandeer(conn=self.scrapli_tn)
136: 
137:         # On fresh ASA, typing 'enable' prompts to set up password
138:         self.logger.debug("Setting up initial enable password")
139:         result = con.send_interactive(
140:             interact_events=[
141:                 ("enable", r"Password:", False),
142:                 (self.password, r"Password:", True),
143:                 # Send an empty character to force the prompt along
144:                 (self.password, r"", False),
145:                 ("", r"ciscoasa#", False),
146:             ],
147:             privilege_level="exec",
148:         )
149: 
150:         self.logger.debug("Entering configuration mode to handle reporting prompt")
151:         result = con.send_interactive(
152:             [
153:                 (
154:                     "configure terminal",
155:                     r"Would you like to enable anonymous error reporting",
156:                     False,
157:                 ),
158:                 ("N", r"(config)#", False),
159:             ]
160:         )
161: 
162:         v4_mgmt_address = vrnetlab.cidr_to_ddn(self.mgmt_address_ipv4)
163:         ipv6_address = self.render_optional_mgmt_config(
164:             "ipv6 address {address}", address=self.mgmt_address_ipv6
165:         )
166:         ipv6_route = self.render_optional_mgmt_config(
167:             "route management ::/0 {gateway} 1", gateway=self.mgmt_gw_ipv6
168:         )
169: 
170:         config_commands = f"""hostname {self.hostname}
171: aaa authentication ssh console LOCAL
172: aaa authentication enable console LOCAL
173: username {self.username} password {self.password} privilege 15
174: interface Management0/0
175: nameif management
176: security-level 100
177: ip address {v4_mgmt_address[0]} {v4_mgmt_address[1]}
178: {ipv6_address}
179: no shutdown
180: exit
181: route management 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 {self.mgmt_gw_ipv4} 1
182: {ipv6_route}
183: access-list MGMT_IN extended permit tcp any any eq ssh
184: access-group MGMT_IN in interface management
185: crypto key generate ecdsa elliptic-curve 256
186: ssh key-exchange group dh-group14-sha256
187: ssh 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 management
188: ssh ::/0 management
189: no ssh stricthostkeycheck
190: ssh timeout 60"""
191: 
192:         self.logger.debug("Sending configuration commands")
193:         con.send_configs(config_commands.splitlines())
194: 
195:         # Apply user-provided startup configuration if present
196:         if os.path.exists(STARTUP_CONFIG_FILE):
197:             self.logger.info("Startup configuration file found")
198:             with open(STARTUP_CONFIG_FILE, "r") as config:
199:                 startup_config = config.read()
200:                 self.logger.debug("Applying startup configuration")
201:                 con.send_configs(startup_config.splitlines())
202:         else:
203:             self.logger.info("User provided startup configuration is not found.")
204: 
205:         self.logger.debug("Saving configuration")
206:         # Exit to privilege exec mode then save
207:         con.acquire_priv("privilege_exec")
208:         con.send_command("write memory")
209:         self.logger.debug("Closing connection")
210:         con.close()
211: 
212: 
213: class ASAv(vrnetlab.VR):
214:     def __init__(self, username, password, conn_mode, hostname):
215:         super(ASAv, self).__init__(username, password)
216:         self.vms = [ASAv_vm(username, password, conn_mode, hostname)]

220:     """ASAv installer"""

<content>
1: # vrnetlab / Cisco ASAv
2: 
3: This is the vrnetlab docker image for Cisco ASAv.
4: 
5: ## Building the docker image
6: 
7: Put the .qcow2 file in this directory and run `make` or `make docker-image` and
8: you should be good to go. The resulting image is called `vrnetlab/cisco_asav:9-24-1`.
9: You can tag it with something else if you want, like `my-repo.example.com/vr-asav` and
10: then push it to your repo. The tag is the same as the version of the ASAv image, so
11: if you have asav9-24-1.qcow2 your final docker image will be called
12: vrnetlab/cisco_asav:9-24-1.
13: 
14: Please note that you will always need to specify version when starting your
15: router as the "latest" tag is not added to any images since it has no meaning
16: in this context.
17: 
18: It's been tested to boot and respond to SSH/telnet with:
19: 
20: - 9.24.1 (asav9-24-1.qcow2) — the ASAv in the CML refplat 2.10
21: - 9.23.1 (asav9-23-1.qcow2) - not tested in the last PR (2026-09)
22: - 9.18.1 (asav9-18-1.qcow2)
23: - 9.9.2 (asav9-9-2.qcow2)
24: 
25: Bootstrap prompts vary between releases, so the launcher answers the ones it is
26: given rather than a fixed sequence.
27: 
28: ## Usage
29: 
30: ```sh
31: # Start a container with the ASAv image. This can take 5-10 minutes to boot
32: docker run -d --privileged --name my-asav-firewall vrnetlab/cisco_asav:9-24-1
33: 
34: # Get the docker container's IP address
35: docker inspect -f '{{range .NetworkSettings.Networks}}{{.IPAddress}}{{end}}' my-asav-firewall
36: 
37: # Follow the boot process, including SSH configuration, this may take a while
38: docker logs -f my-asav-firewall
39: 
40: # After the ASAv has booted, SSH to it using the configured credentials
41: ssh admin@<docker-ip> # password: CiscoAsa1!
42: 
43: # Alternatively, you can connect to the console with telnet
44: telnet <docker-ip> 5000
45: ```
46: 
47: ## Forced password change on first login
48: 
49: Recent releases (9.18.1 and 9.24.1 here, not 9.9.2) mark a user whose password was
50: set by an administrator as "New-User" and force the first authenticated login to
51: change it. Nothing in the configuration clears it and it cannot be turned off — see
52: [this thread](https://community.cisco.com/t5/network-security/asa-9-19-forced-password-changes/td-p/4833624).
53: 
54: Only authenticated logins get the dialog, so the launcher briefly authenticates the
55: console, answers it with the same password, and removes the console authentication
56: again. You get a device you can log straight in to.
57: 
58: ## Interface mapping
59: 
60: Management0/0 is always configured as a management interface, and the table below maps
61: the container's data interfaces (`eth1`, `eth2`, ...) to the ASAv ones.
62: 
63: It is the full mapping, not what every node gets: a NIC is only created for a container
64: interface that exists, so a node with two links has Management0/0, GigabitEthernet0/0 and
65: GigabitEthernet0/1 and nothing else.
66: 
67: | vr-asav             | vr-xcon |
68: | :---:               |  :---:  |
69: | Management0/0       | 0       |
70: | GigabitEthernet0/0  | 1       |
71: | GigabitEthernet0/1  | 2       |
72: | GigabitEthernet0/2  | 3       |
73: | GigabitEthernet0/3  | 4       |
74: | GigabitEthernet0/4  | 5       |
75: | GigabitEthernet0/5  | 6       |
76: | GigabitEthernet0/6  | 7       |
77: | GigabitEthernet0/7  | 8       |
78: 
79: ## System requirements
80: 
81: CPU: 1 core
82: 
83: RAM: 2GB
84: 
85: Disk: <500MB
86: 
87: ## FUAQ - Frequently or Unfrequently Asked Questions
88: 
89: ### Q: Has this been extensively tested?
90: 
91: A: Nope. Not really, in September 2026, the version available in CML: 9.24.1 was tested successfully. Same for 9.18.1.
92: 9.9.2 was briefly tested, i.e. the container is healthy, but no further tests were performed.

(End of file - total 92 lines)

150:     def render_optional_mgmt_config(self, template, **values):
151:         """Render static management config only when all values are available."""
152:         if not all(value and value != "dhcp" for value in values.values()):
153:             return ""
154: 
155:         for name, value in values.items():
156:             template = template.replace(f"{{{name}}}", str(value))
157: 
158:         return template
159: 
160:     def _overlay_disk_image_format(self) -> str:
161:         res = run_command(["qemu-img", "info", "--output", "json", self.image])
162:         if res is not None:
163:             image_info = json.loads(res[0])
164:             if "format" in image_info:
165:                 return image_info["format"]
166:         raise ValueError(f"Could not read image format for {self.image}")
167: 
168:     def __init__(
169:         self,
170:         username,
171:         password,
172:         disk_image="",
173:         num=0,
174:         ram=4096,
175:         driveif="ide",
176:         provision_pci_bus=True,
177:         cpu="host",
178:         smp="1",
179:         mgmt_passthrough=False,
180:         mgmt_intf="eth0",
181:         mgmt_dhcp=False,
182:         min_dp_nics=0,
183:         data_intf_prefix="eth",
184:         arch="x86_64",
185:     ):
186:         self.arch = arch
187: 
188:         # configure logging
189:         self.logger = logging.getLogger()
190: 
191:         """
192:         Configure Scrapli logger to only be INFO level.
193:         Scrapli uses 'scrapli' logger by default, and
194:         will write all channel i/o as DEBUG log level.
195:         """
196:         self.scrapli_logger = logging.getLogger("scrapli")
197: 
198:         scrapli_log_level = (
199:             logging.DEBUG
200:             if os.getenv("DEBUG_SCRAPLI", "false").lower() == "true"
201:             else logging.INFO
202:         )
203:         self.scrapli_logger.setLevel(scrapli_log_level)
204: 
205:         # init scrapli_tn -- main telnet device
206:         scrapli_tn_dev = {
207:             "host": "127.0.0.1",
208:             "port": 5000 + num,
209:             "auth_bypass": True,
210:             "auth_strict_key": False,
211:             "transport": "telnet",
212:             "timeout_socket": 3600,
213:             "timeout_transport": 3600,
214:             "timeout_ops": 3600,
215:         }
216: 
217:         self.scrapli_tn = Driver(**scrapli_tn_dev)
218: 
219:         # init scrapli_qm_dev -- qemu monitor device
220:         scrapli_qm_dev = {
221:             "host": "127.0.0.1",
222:             "port": 4000 + num,
223:             "auth_bypass": True,
224:             "auth_strict_key": False,
225:             "transport": "telnet",
226:             "timeout_socket": 3600,
227:             "timeout_transport": 3600,
228:             "timeout_ops": 3600,
229:         }
230: 
231:         self.scrapli_qm = Driver(**scrapli_qm_dev)
232: 
233:         # telnetlib-compatible facades the launchers use: self.tn drives the
234:         # serial console, self.qm the qemu monitor.
235:         self.tn = _Console(self.scrapli_tn)
236:         self.qm = _Console(self.scrapli_qm)
237: 
238:         # username / password to configure
239:         self.username = username
240:         self.password = password
241: 
242:         self.num = num
243:         self.image = disk_image
244: 
245:         self.running = False
246:         self.spins = 0
247:         self.p = None
248: 
249:         self._ram = ram
250:         self._cpu = cpu
251:         self._smp = smp
252:         self.mgmt_intf = os.environ.get("CLAB_MGMT_INTF", mgmt_intf)
253: 
254:         # various settings
255:         # start() appends the UUID resolved by the uuid property to the qemu command.
256:         self._uuid = None
257:         self.fake_start_date = None
258:         self.nic_type = "e1000"
259:         self.num_nics = 0
260:         # number of nics that are actually *provisioned* (as in nics that will be added to container)
261:         self.num_provisioned_nics = int(os.environ.get("CLAB_INTFS", 0))
262:         # "highest" provisioned nic num -- used for making sure we can allocate nics without needing
263:         # to have them allocated sequential from eth1
264:         self.highest_provisioned_nic_num = 0
265: 
266:         # Whether the management interface is pass-through or host-forwarded.
267:         # Host-forwarded is the original vrnetlab mode where a VM gets a static IP for its management address,
268:         # which **does not** match the eth0 interface of a container.
269:         # In pass-through mode the VM container uses the same IP as the container's eth0 interface and transparently forwards traffic between the two interfaces.
270:         # See https://github.com/hellt/vrnetlab/issues/286
271:         self.mgmt_passthrough = (
272:             os.environ.get("CLAB_MGMT_PASSTHROUGH", "").lower() == "true"
273:             if os.environ.get("CLAB_MGMT_PASSTHROUGH")
274:             else mgmt_passthrough
275:         )
276: 
277:         # Check if CLAB_MGMT_DHCP environment variable is set
278:         self.mgmt_dhcp = (
279:             os.environ.get("CLAB_MGMT_DHCP", "").lower() == "true"
280:             if os.environ.get("CLAB_MGMT_DHCP")
281:             else mgmt_dhcp
282:         )
283: 
284:         # Check if CLAB_INTF_PREFIX environment variable is set
285:         self.data_intf_prefix = os.environ.get("CLAB_INTF_PREFIX", data_intf_prefix)
286: 
287:         # Populate management IP and gateway
288:         # If CLAB_MGMT_DHCP environment variable is set, we assume that a DHCP client
289:         # inside of the VM will take care about setting the management IP and gateway.
290:         if self.mgmt_passthrough:
291:             if self.mgmt_dhcp:
292:                 self.mgmt_address_ipv4 = "dhcp"
293:                 self.mgmt_address_ipv6 = "dhcp"
294:                 self.mgmt_gw_ipv4 = "dhcp"
295:                 self.mgmt_gw_ipv6 = "dhcp"
296:             else:
297:                 self.mgmt_address_ipv4, self.mgmt_address_ipv6 = self.get_mgmt_address()
298:                 self.mgmt_gw_ipv4, self.mgmt_gw_ipv6 = self.get_mgmt_gw()
299:         else:

Let me find scrapli's Channel.read source. I know scrapli's sync channel:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!

        This method will *not* look for a prompt and will simply read all data available on the
        channel.

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes as read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if the transport read times out
        """
        with self._channel_lock:
            buf = b""
            while True:
                try:
                    buf += self.transport.read()
                except ScrapliTimeout:
                    break
            return buf
```

Reasoning
scrapli 2024.7.30.post1. Let me recall scrapli 2024.x `Channel.read()`:

From scrapli source (channel/channel.py, sync), version 2024.7.30:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!

        This method will *not* look for a prompt, and will simply read all data available on the
        channel. This is a low-level method that you probably should not use unless you know what
        you are doing.

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes as read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if a read timeout occurs
        """
        with self._channel_lock:
            buf = b""
            while True:
                try:
                    buf += self.transport.read()
                except ScrapliTimeout:
                    break
            return buf
```

Hmm, I really do remember `read()` being documented as "Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!". And there's `read_until_prompt()` which raises ScrapliTimeout on timeout. And `read_until_expects`.

Actually, I found it. scrapli/channel/channel.py (2024.x):

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!

        This method will *not* look for a prompt, and will simply read all data available on the
        channel. This is a low-level method, you probably want `read_until_prompt` or one of the
        `send_*` methods instead.

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes as read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if the transport read times out
        """
        with self._channel_lock:
            buf = b""
            while True:
                try:
                    buf += self.transport.read()
                except ScrapliTimeout:
                    self.transport.close()
                    raise  # ???
```

No. Let me instead reason from vrnetlab.py's own usage — lines 1133-1163 use `self.scrapli_tn.channel.read()` inside con_expect with wrapper timeout handling; and line 314 of cumulus: "scrapli channel.read() can block past the wrapper timeout." Let me read those sections — the established in-repo usage will tell how vrnetlab handles read timeouts (they must catch ScrapliTimeout there, proving it raises).

Let me read vrnetlab.py around lines 80-130 (_Console) and 1100-1200 (con_expect).

Reasoning
Key observation from `_Console._read()` and `con_expect`: they call `self.scrapli_tn.channel.read()` in a loop with `time.sleep(0.1)` when `data` is empty — this strongly implies scrapli's `Channel.read()` does NOT block until prompt; it returns whatever is available and may return empty bytes. That is, scrapli's `Channel.read()` in 2024.x is a non-blocking-ish "read all currently available data" — it loops `while True: buf += transport.read()` breaking on ScrapliTimeout? No wait — if it blocked until transport timeout (3600s in scrapli_tn), then `_Console.expect` with `time.sleep(0.1)` after empty data would never be reached until 3600s. And `con_expect([b"ciscoasa>"], 1)` with timeout=1 — it loops `while time.time() < t_end: buf += channel.read()` — with a 1-second wrapper timeout, meaning each `channel.read()` must return quickly. So indeed scrapli `Channel.read()` returns promptly with available data (it does a single transport read with short/0 timeout? or it breaks on timeout quickly).

Hmm, but wait — the telnet transport read uses `timeout_socket`/`timeout_transport`. The scrapli_tn has timeout_transport=3600. If `Channel.read()` did a single blocking `transport.read()` (blocking until 3600s timeout), `con_expect(..., 1)` would hang forever. But con_expect works — it's used in bootstrap_spin with 1s timeout across all launchers. So `Channel.read()` must return available data without blocking the full transport timeout. How? In scrapli, `Channel.read()`:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        buf = b""
        while True:
            try:
                buf += self.transport.read()
            except ScrapliTimeout:
                break
        return buf
```

That would block until a timeout... with timeout_transport=3600 that's 3600s. Unless... hmm, but maybe the telnet transport `read` in scrapli: `TelnetTransport.read()` — uses `self.telnet_client.read_very_eager()`? Actually scrapli's telnet transport wraps telnetlib3? No, scrapli's telnet transport is a synchronous implementation over sockets with `select`? Let me think: scrapli transport base `read()` for socket transports does `sock.recv(...)` with socket timeout — blocking. But `Channel.read()` semantics "read all data available without looking for a prompt" — with a blocking socket recv, it would block until timeout.

Hmm, but the evidence from vrnetlab's `_Console` (lines 111-124): `read_until` loops calling `self._read()` and sleeps 0.1 when empty. And `con_expect` with timeout loops `channel.read()`. These are pre-existing code paths that work in production (they're how all VMs boot). So `channel.read()` must return promptly (probably empty) when no data is available. 

How? In scrapli's sync telnet transport, `read()` might use `select` with the socket timeout... Actually wait — I recall scrapli's telnet transport `read()`:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        if self.eof:
            raise EOFError
        ...
        return self.telnet_client.read_very_eager()  # non-blocking!
```

No... scrapli's telnet transport is implemented in `scrapli/transport/plugins/telnet/telnet_transport.py` with its own `_TelnetClient` that uses non-blocking reads via select. Yes! scrapli's telnet transport uses a custom telnet client with `select.select` and reads whatever is available. Its `read()` returns available data without blocking indefinitely? Hmm, but then how does `timeout_transport` apply?

Actually, I recall scrapli's telnet transport `read()`:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read as much data as possible from the channel
        """
        if self.eof:
            raise EOFError
        ...
        read_data = b""
        ...
        try:
            read_data += self.telnet_client.read()
        ...
```

and `_TelnetClient.read()` uses select with a timeout, returning b"" on timeout? Hmm.

OK, empirical in-repo evidence is strong: `channel.read()` returns promptly (possibly empty). The cumulus comment "scrapli channel.read() can block past the wrapper timeout" suggests it CAN block, though — let me read that section to understand.

class Channel(ChannelBase):
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!

        This method will *not* look for a prompt, and will simply read all data available on the
        channel. This is a very low level method that you probably don't want to use unless you
        know what you are doing.

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes as read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if the transport read times out
        """

            buf = b""
            while True:
                try:
                    buf += self.transport.read()
                except ScrapliTimeout:
                    break
            return buf
```

OK, let me stop trying to recall and instead consider what the PR authors themselves wrote — their comment is the best evidence of observed behavior:

"A read that times out takes the transport down with it, so it has to be kept short enough to not stall the boot for the whole transport timeout."

This says: a read CAN time out, and when it does, the transport goes down. And the time a read takes before timing out is the transport timeout — which they reduced from 3600 (the commandeered value from scrapli_tn: `timeout_transport: 3600`) to `CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT` (60s), because otherwise a timed-out read would "stall the boot for the whole transport timeout" (3600s).

So per the authors: reads block up to `timeout_transport` and, on timeout, kill the transport. That means `channel.read()` blocks until either data arrives... no wait — if it returned as soon as ANY data arrived, then during a quiet wait for a prompt it would block up to timeout. So semantics: `channel.read()` blocks until the transport read times out (returning accumulated data? or raising?).

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!

        This method will *not* look for a prompt, and will simply read all data available on the
        channel. This is a low level method that you probably should not use unless you have a
        good reason to.

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes as read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if the transport read times out

        with self._channel_lock:

            while True:
                try:
                    buf += self.transport.read()
                except ScrapliTimeout:
                    break
            return buf
```

With this, `channel.read()` blocks until the transport read times out (i.e., quiet console → full timeout_transport) OR... no wait, the while loop: first transport.read() blocks until data OR timeout. If data arrives (console output), it returns immediately → buf += data → loop again → second transport.read() blocks until timeout → break. So `channel.read()` returns all data available NOW plus waits one transport-timeout period for more. With timeout_transport=3600, EVERY call to channel.read() would block 3600s after data stops. 5. **The `_console_dialog` empty-read busy loop**: if `channel.read()` returns empty promptly (non-blocking-ish), the `while time.time() < deadline` loop becomes a busy CPU loop with no sleep when the console is quiet — spinning for up to 60s per dialog call, and with several dialogs (2 in apply_config + 2 in complete_first_login = 4), up to 240s of 100% CPU busy-waiting per boot in the worst case (when prompts don't appear). Compare `_Console.read_until`/`expect` which `time.sleep(0.1)` when no data. This is a genuine, verifiable-by-snapshot issue: no sleep in the loop. If `channel.read()` blocks until timeout (60s each), then the deadline loop would make at most 2 iterations (60+60s) — worst case ~120s per dialog, and the transport dies on the first timeout (per the comment), so subsequent operations fail. Either way, there's an issue:

   - If read returns promptly when quiet → busy spin, 100% CPU for the dialog duration (60s × up to 4 dialogs), plus fine behavior otherwise.
   - If read blocks until timeout_transport → on quiet console, first read raises/returns after 60s; if it raises ScrapliTimeout, `_console_dialog` propagates (apply_config dialogs unprotected) → boot failure; if it returns, the deadline is already exceeded → our ScrapliTimeout raised, and the transport is dead per the authors' comment.

   Which is it? The `_Console.expect` evidence (`sleep(0.1)` on empty data) suggests `channel.read()` returns promptly-empty when quiet. So the busy loop is real. Actually wait — does `_Console.expect` get called when the console is quiet? `con_expect` in bootstrap_spin: `while time.time() < t_end: buf += channel.read()` — the timeout=1 loop. If channel.read() blocked 3600s when quiet, this would hang. Since bootstrap works for all VMs in production (this is base code, unchanged), channel.read() must return quickly when quiet. Hmm — unless... `self.scrapli_tn.channel.read()` — ChannelBase.read? Hmm, wait: maybe scrapli's `Channel.read()` is:

   ```python
   def read(self) -> bytes:
       return self._read_buf_extended()  
   ```

   Hmm, whatever — the empirical evidence from the working base code settles it: `channel.read()` returns promptly (possibly empty) on a quiet telnet console. (scrapli's telnet transport uses a select() with a very short timeout — actually I do now recall scrapli's telnet transport `_TelnetClient.read()` doing `select.select([self.sock], [], [], 0)` style non-blocking reads, raising ScrapliTimeout only via an idle mechanism. Yes — scrapli telnet transport is non-blocking by design! That's why vrnetlab wraps it this way and why `timeout_transport=3600` doesn't block con_expect.    OK so with non-blocking reads: `_console_dialog`'s loop is a **busy-wait** with no sleep. Now, the key remaining question for the review: the semantics of `channel.read()` blocking. Let me try once more to pin down scrapli 2024.7.30's Channel.read. I have decent memory of scrapli code. scrapli/channel/channel.py (sync):

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read and return all data from the channel without looking for a prompt!

        This method will *not* look for a prompt, and will simply read all data available on the
        channel. This is a low level method, you probably want to use `read_until_prompt` or
        `send_input` and friends instead.

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes as read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if the transport read times out

        """
        with self._channel_lock:
            buf = b""
            while True:
                try:
                    buf += self.transport.read()
                except ScrapliTimeout:
                    break
            return buf
```

Hmm — if that's right, then with the telnet transport: does `transport.read()` block until data or timeout? scrapli's telnet transport `read()`:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read as much data as possible from the channel

        This read is a little bit "special" because of the telnet negotiation... it reads all
        available data. If there is no data available, it waits for... 
        """
        if self.eof:
            raise EOFError
        buf = b""
        while True:
            ...
            try:
                buf += self.telnet_client.read()
            except (EOFError, ScrapliTimeout):
                break
```

The scrapli telnet transport uses `_TelnetClient` with a socket and select; its `read()` does `select.select([self.sock], [], [], self._read_timeout?)`. Honestly, I recall scrapli telnet transport has a `read_timeout`/idle mechanism: after the socket has been idle... 

Let me look at vrnetlab's `_Console.expect` again: it does `data = self._read()`; `if not data: time.sleep(0.1)`. If `channel.read()` blocked until transport timeout (3600) when quiet, the `if not data: sleep(0.1)` branch would be dead code and `read_until(timeout)` would never honor its timeout. The presence of this code across the repo (and its production use) is strong evidence that `channel.read()` returns promptly-empty when the telnet console is quiet.

But then the PR comment "A read that times out takes the transport down with it" — contradicts "returns promptly"? Unless: `channel.read()` returns promptly with available data, and the transport-level timeout only triggers when the socket itself is dead (no connection), raising ScrapliTimeout which "takes the transport down". I.e., reads time out only in pathological cases, and that kills the transport. Hmm, but then why would reducing `timeout_transport` from 3600 to 60 matter ("stall the boot for the whole transport timeout")? Because a timed-out read stalls for timeout_transport... so reads DO block up to timeout_transport in some case. When? When data never arrives — i.e., the quiet console case!

So both behaviors coexist: `channel.read()` blocks when no data is available, up to timeout_transport. Then `con_expect(timeout=1)` would block... UNLESS the loop breaks early. `con_expect`: `while time.time() < t_end: buf += self.scrapli_tn.channel.read()`. If channel.read() blocks 3600s, con_expect(1) hangs 3600s. This is base code that demonstrably works (this is how all vrnetlab VMs boot). Therefore channel.read() does NOT block 3600s when the console is quiet.

Resolution: scrapli's telnet transport `read()` must return promptly (b"" or available data) regardless of timeout_transport — e.g., select with tiny timeout — and `timeout_transport` for telnet is used elsewhere. Then what "times out" per the authors' comment? Maybe: when the connection is dead/unresponsive, `transport.read()` raises ScrapliTimeout after timeout_transport, and scrapli's channel kills the transport on that. And the authors, when testing, encountered such reads taking 3600s? That would mean con_expect DOES sometimes block for 3600s... and vrnetlab's `bootstrap_spin` calls con_expect with timeout=1 — if channel.read() blocked for 3600s there, boot would stall 3600s. Hmm, but maybe con_expect is only reached when... no, it's called every loop iteration.

I can't fully resolve this. Honestly, maybe scrapli's telnet transport read is:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        ...
        buf = b""
        while not buf:
            buf = self._telnet_client.read(very_eager?) 
            ...
```

You know what — let me look at how OTHER launchers in this repo handle read timeouts around `channel.read()` to see whether exceptions are expected. The cumulus comment says "scrapli channel.read() can block past the wrapper timeout" — implying channel.read() CAN block, and the workaround was to avoid read_until after a certain point. And huawei_vrp or nokia sros may catch ScrapliTimeout. Let me grep for "read_until_prompt", "ScrapliTimeout", "except Timeout" in launchers.

Let me also check nokia/sros launch.py line ~1306 area for commandeer + read patterns, and see if any launcher catches exceptions around channel.read.

Reasoning
No launcher catches `ScrapliTimeout` around `channel.read()` elsewhere. The evidence from `_Console` suggests `channel.read()` returns promptly-empty when quiet (otherwise the entire vrnetlab boot loop, which does `con_expect(..., 1)` in a spin loop, would stall for the 3600s transport timeout). I'll treat "read returns promptly with available data" as the operative semantics (consistent with scrapli telnet transport using non-blocking eager reads).

Given that, in `_console_dialog`:
- The while loop spins without sleep when no data arrives → hot CPU loop for up to 60s per dialog (and up to 4 dialogs per boot; in the failure path of complete_first_login, an extra recovery send_configs also happens). The pre-existing `_Console.expect`/`read_until` sleep 0.1 when data is empty. This is a concrete P3 performance finding.

Wait — one more consideration: does `channel.read()` return b"" promptly on the telnet transport when quiet? Look at `con_expect`: `while time.time() < t_end: buf += self.scrapli_tn.channel.read()` — with `timeout=1`. And also `read_very_eager` wraps `_read()` = `channel.read()`. And `_Console.read_until` sleeps 0.1 when empty. All consistent with prompt return.

Hmm, but hold on — there's a counter-consideration: scrapli's `Channel.read()` might loop `while True` reading until a read returns empty, breaking on ScrapliTimeout. For a telnet transport where `read()` returns b"" when nothing available (non-blocking), the loop would spin internally until it gets data? If transport.read() returns b"" immediately, the while loop `buf += b""` spins forever (no break on empty!) unless there's an idle check. So `channel.read()` can't just break on empty — it would infinite-loop. So scrapli's channel.read must either (a) block via transport read until timeout, or (b) do a single read and return.

Option (b): `Channel.read()` = single `transport.read()` call + some post-processing (e.g., filter telnet negotiation). Then behavior depends on transport.read() blocking behavior. scrapli's telnet transport `read()`: I'm now fairly sure it uses select with the socket timeout... The socket for telnet is created with `sock.settimeout(timeout_socket)`? In scrapli telnet transport, `open` creates socket and calls `sock.settimeout(timeout_socket?)`. Hmm — but con_expect(timeout=1) then could block up to timeout_socket=3600. Contradiction again.

Unless... scrapli's telnet transport read returns b"" quickly! Let me recall the actual telnet transport code (scrapli/transport/plugins/telnet/telnet_transport.py). I remember it well enough now, actually:

```python
class TelnetTransport(PluginTransport):
    ...
    def open(self):
        ...
        self.sock = socket.create_connection((self.host, self.port), timeout=self.timeout_socket)
        self.sock.settimeout(0.1)?? 
```

Hmm. And:

```python
    def read(self) -> bytes:
        """
        Read as much data as possible from the channel

        Returns:
            bytes: bytes read from the channel

        Raises:
            ScrapliTimeout: if the transport read times out
        """
        if self.eof:
            raise EOFError
        buf = b""
        while True:
            try:
                buf += self._telnet_client.read_very_eager()
            except EOFError:
                break
            ...
```

I give up on exact recall. The empirical route: this PR was presumably tested against 9.24.1 successfully (README claims). If channel.read() blocked for 60s every iteration (with the reduced timeout), a successful boot with 4+ dialog calls each doing ≥2 reads would take 480+ seconds of blocking — possible, since boot takes 5-10 minutes anyway and bootstrap_spin's con_expect loop... wait no, con_expect is BEFORE apply_config; during apply_config the reads are only from _console_dialog and scrapli's internal ops (which use read_until_prompt with timeout_ops=scrapli_timeout=900 by default... hmm interesting — DEFAULT_SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT=900? Let me check line 23: `DEFAULT_SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT = 900`. Hmm, but wait — asa_scrapli_dev sets timeout_socket/transport/ops = scrapli_timeout from SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT env or 900s. Hmm, but that's con's own timeouts — but con.commandeer(conn=self.scrapli_tn) — per the authors' comment, commandeer "kept the timeouts of the connection it came from" — so con's configured timeouts are overridden by scrapli_tn's 3600s? That's the whole reason for the transport_args hack. OK.

So under "read blocks until timeout_transport": during apply_config, each `_console_dialog` read would block 60s. For the enable dialog on 9.24.1: send "enable" → device echoes "enable\r\n" and asks... on 9.24.1 with no enable password: "Enter Password: " etc. First read blocks... but data arrives quickly — if read blocks only UNTIL data arrives, then returns promptly. That's the natural socket semantics: `sock.recv` with timeout T returns as soon as data is available; times out after T if nothing. So `transport.read()` blocks until data OR timeout. Then scrapli's channel.read() looping `while True: buf += transport.read() except ScrapliTimeout: break` → returns all data plus one timeout period of waiting. With quiet console → blocks the full timeout_transport (60s here, 3600s for scrapli_tn in con_expect!).

CONTRADICTION with con_expect(1) again... unless con_expect's blocking is acceptable because bootstrap_spin's con_expect is only reached when... no, bootstrap_spin calls con_expect([b"ciscoasa>"], 1) repeatedly in the work() loop — if each call blocked 3600s, the "spin > 300 → restart VM" logic would never trigger and boot stall detection would be broken. In production this works (VM restarts happen on failed boots, and logs show spins incrementing). So channel.read() must NOT block the full transport timeout when quiet.

=> Therefore scrapli's Channel.read() must do a single non-blocking-ish read returning promptly. Therefore `con_expect(timeout=1)` works: each iteration reads available data quickly. And therefore, in `_console_dialog`, when the console is quiet, `channel.read()` returns b"" promptly → the while loop hot-spins → 100% CPU for up to 60s per dialog. And the "read that times out takes the transport down" refers to a pathological case where the transport's read eventually raises (e.g., connection dead) — with timeout_transport governing that pathological wait.

Hmm, but then how would a read "stall the boot for the whole transport timeout" (3600s)? Only in the pathological dead-connection case. The authors observed this? Plausible — e.g., when the device never responds because the telnet connection is half-open.

OK. So my P3 finding: missing sleep in the dialog wait loop → hot spin for up to `CONSOLE_DIALOG_TIMEOUT` seconds when a prompt never arrives (the "release does not ask" path and failure paths). With 4 dialog calls per boot, up to ~4 minutes of busy-waiting in the worst case (2 dialogs in apply_config typically return quickly on matched releases; the worst cases are failure paths). Actually, more precisely: in successful flows, each dialog returns quickly. Failure flows: `complete_first_login` dialogs that time out spin hot 60s each (2 × 60s = 120s hot), plus recovery. And in `apply_config`, unexpected prompts → hot 60s then crash. So the hot spin occurs in the "release doesn't ask" paths — e.g., 9.9.2's complete_first_login: "exit" → does 9.9.2 (no forced change, but serial console auth enabled) prompt Username: on exit? With `aaa authentication serial console LOCAL` configured, exiting exec on the serial console → ASA prompts "Username:". I believe yes — so exec_prompt match requires a successful login: username + password → `asa>`. Fine.

Hmm wait, actually there's a subtlety on 9.9.2: `aaa authentication serial console LOCAL` — after "exit" from exec prompt, ASA may print "Logoff" and then... if the user session ends, the console shows "Username:". Good.

So the hot spin happens mainly on failure/unexpected-prompt paths and on releases where dialogs complete fast. I'll report it as P3 (or P4?). The impact: bounded 60s × N dialogs of 100% CPU in a container — modest, but easily avoided by the sleep(0.1) used in the repo's own `_Console`. I'll call it P3? It's a bounded inefficiency, not correctness. P4 seems right per "optional actionable improvement"... but it's a real defect-ish (CPU spin). The severity definitions: P3 minor defect. A hot loop consuming CPU on every boot path that waits — I'd say P3.

Wait — actually, I realize I should double check: does scrapli's Channel.read() have a lock (`self._channel_lock`)? If `channel.read()` acquires a lock... irrelevant here.

Next: **the un-anchored `Password:` answer in apply_config's enable dialog could mis-fire during `complete_first_login`?** No, different dialogs.

**Another potential P2: `complete_first_login` runs even when the install_mode is used?** No — apply_config is only called in non-install mode.

**Reboot behavior**: startup config contains `aaa authentication enable console LOCAL` (from config_commands) — so after reboot, the console... the ASAv serial console is not authenticated at boot (no serial auth in saved config) — good, the container's console (telnet 5000) stays accessible for `docker logs`-style debugging. Consistent with base.

**Check: after `complete_first_login` removes serial auth, is there any chance the device prompts "Password:" for enable after that? The "enable" dialog consumed that. Fine.

**Potential missed case in complete_first_login**: after answering the forced-change dialog, does ASA present the exec prompt `hostname>` or does it re-ask? Assume prompt. If the release prints a different post-login state (e.g., an auto "show version"?) — out of scope.

**Now a genuinely suspicious one: `_console_dialog(con, "exit", exec_prompt, answers)` — answers include `([Uu]sername:) → username`. But what if the device does NOT ask for authentication on "exit" because the serial console auth command wasn't applied (send_configs failed silently?)? Then "exit" from exec prompt... on ASA, exiting exec mode on the serial console when session started via serial? The console session: the launcher talks over the qemu serial console (telnet 5000). The session is "the console" — with serial console auth, exit → "Username:". Without → what does "exit" from `hostname#` do on ASA console? It logs out and shows... the same console prompts "Username:"? Actually on ASA, after `exit` from exec on serial console, you get the console "Username:" prompt only if serial auth is configured; otherwise the console just shows the standard login banner "ciscoasa> " again? Hmm — with `aaa authentication serial console LOCAL` NOT configured, the console lets you straight back in to `ciscoasa>` exec. So if send_configs failed to apply, "exit" lands at `hostname>` anyway → exec_prompt matches → "enable" → `hostname#`... wait but without console auth, "enable" asks "Password:" (enable secret — the enable password set in apply_config... wait, was an enable password set? The enable dialog sets it: for 9.12+ releases, "Enter Password:"/"Repeat Password:" set the enable password to self.password. For older releases, the generic "" answer — hmm, that means no enable password was set on older releases, but `aaa authentication enable console LOCAL` in the config means enable authentication goes through LOCAL (the username's password) — so "enable" prompts "Password:" → answered with self.password (the username's password) → works via LOCAL. Fine.)

**Now — the bigger-picture check on `complete_first_login` for releases where the user is NOT flagged (9.9.2)**: `aaa authentication serial console LOCAL` is added to the RUNNING config AFTER `write memory` — so it never persists. Then removed. Net effect on saved config: none. 

**But here's an issue: the running-config change `aaa authentication serial console LOCAL` happens AFTER `write memory` — if the bootstrap subsequently crashes (e.g., in the recovery path send_configs fails), the running config retains serial auth but that's not saved. Fine.

**Another: `complete_first_login` catches only (ScrapliException, OSError). If the device's dialog never completes and `_console_dialog` raises ScrapliTimeout (a ScrapliException) — caught. Good.

**Now, a REAL bug candidate in `_console_dialog`'s answer loop**: `con.channel.write(reply, redacted=True)` — for the generic `[Pp]assword:` answer in the enable dialog, reply is "" — `write("")` then `send_return()` → sends "\r". OK matches base behavior.

**Edge: the `matched` min ordering when two patterns match at the same start**: e.g., buf = "... Enter Password:" — "Enter\s+Password:" matches at 6? and "Password:" matches at (6 + len("Enter ")?) Hmm: "Enter Password:" — `[Ee]nter\s+Password:` matches starting at 'E' (index 0 of "Enter"), "Password:" matches starting at 6. min picks (0,...) — the specific one. Good. But what about `Repeat\s+Password:` vs `Enter\s+Password:`? Different starts. Fine.

But consider `complete_first_login` answers: buf = "Enter old password: " — matches "[Ee]nter old password" at 0 and "[Pp]assword:" at 10 → min → specific. Good. buf = "Password: " → only generic. Good.

**What about the case where the device echoes the username we typed, e.g., "Username: admin" — then later "Password:"? The buf was truncated after "Username:", so the echo arrives in the next read; the tail contains "admin\r\nPassword: " — hmm wait, when we write the username, the device echoes it. The next `channel.read()` returns "admin\r\nPassword: " — wait, no: we wrote the reply; the echo of what we typed appears in subsequent reads. The buf contains "admin" — does any answer regex match "admin"? `[Uu]sername:` — no ("Username:" was consumed and truncated). Fine.

**Now `apply_config`'s first dialog `prompt` = `re.compile(r"ciscoasa#")` — unanchored, and the device echo of "enable" is "enable\r\n" — no "#". Fine. But what if the release 9.24.1, after the enable password setup, shows "ciscoasa#" — yes.

**Potential issue with `ciscoasa#` prompt matching when the hostname was changed by... not yet.

OK now let me also check the README claim "run `make` or `make docker-image`" — check the asav Makefile to see if `make` (default target) builds docker-image.
